@@139060081 <h> Funny <h> Growing avocado supply brings greater uncertainty <p> The global avocado market is entering a new phase . Structural growth in supply is continuing , but climate change , geopolitical developments , logistical disruptions , and periods of oversupply are making the market increasingly unpredictable , according to RaboResearch . <p> Global avocado supply continues to riseGlobal avocado export volumes have more than quadrupled over the past fifteen years , rising from around 0.73 million tons in 2010 to an expected 3.8 million tons in 2029 ( Figure 1 ) . Mexico and Peru remain the leading exporting countries , but Colombia , Kenya , Spain , South Africa , Morocco , and other origins are becoming increasingly important . This diversification of supply makes the market more resilient , but it also intensifies competition between producing regions and puts pressure on prices when several origins are supplying the European market at the same time . <p> European avocado market continues to grow , but more promotion and planning are needed to manage the summer peakFor Europe , the growth trend remains clear . The EU-27 is the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Since 2010 , European imports have increased almost fivefold . Avocado availability in the EU reached around 2.24 kilograms per capita in 2025 , roughly half the U.S. level ( Figure 2 ) . This indicates further growth potential , particularly in major consumer markets such as Germany and Italy . According to calculations , an additional annual consumption of half an avocado per person ( approximately 100 grams ) could increase EU demand to around 1.47 million tons by 2035 . This presents a clear opportunity for Dutch traders , provided that growth is distributed more evenly throughout the year . <p> Seasonal concentration remains a bottleneck in the European market . The EU receives large volumes of avocados during the summer months , mainly due to Peru 's peak export season . In July 2025 , EU imports exceeded 110,000 tons , equivalent to more than 6.5 million four-kilo boxes per week . During the winter , imports averaged around 4.4 million boxes per week . This imbalance puts pressure on prices during the summer . Once the Peruvian peak has passed , prices often recover quickly @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , timing and targeted promotional activities are therefore just as important as volume . <p> South America remains the growth driver , despite weather risksSouth America continues to drive avocado supply growth in Europe . In 2025 , the region surpassed 1 million tons of avocado exports for the first time . Peru , Colombia , and a recovering Chile led this growth . Peru remains on track to potentially exceed 1 million tons of exports by 2030 , although low prices and weather-related risks could slow that trajectory . Although still well behind Peru , Colombia continues to expand rapidly , with exports estimated at around 190,000 tons in 2025/26 , up 16 per cent from the previous season . Chile recorded its highest export volume in sixteen seasons , supported by a recovery following years of water scarcity . Brazil is also expanding rapidly , with exports expected to reach 40,000 tons in 2026 , around 60 per cent more than in 2025 . <p> At the same time , El Ni ? o is making South American supply less predictable . In Peru , warmer and wetter @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ drop , quality issues , and variation in fruit size . Colombia may experience warmer and drier conditions during critical flowering periods , which could affect yields and fruit grading . For Dutch importers , this means they must prepare for greater variation in quality , size , and availability , even as overall regional volumes continue to increase . <p> African avocados are gaining ground despite vulnerable supply chainsMeanwhile , Africa is evolving from a supplementary source region into a strategic pillar for risk diversification . African avocado exports reached approximately 450,000 tons in 2025 , an increase of 16 per cent compared with 2024 . Kenya , Morocco , and South Africa remain key suppliers , while emerging exporters such as Tanzania and Burundi are becoming increasingly important ( Figure 5 ) . Export windows are also expanding . Traditionally , African shipments peaked in March and April , but since 2022 , export activity between September and February has increased significantly , mainly driven by Morocco , Burundi , and other producing countries . This is particularly relevant for Dutch importers seeking to strengthen supplies for the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ sector remains vulnerable . The region depends heavily on exports to overseas markets and is highly exposed to logistical disruptions . Disruptions affecting the Red Sea and the Strait of Hormuz have increased both costs and uncertainty . Europe remains by far the most important export market for African avocados , accounting for an average of 82 per cent of export value over the past five years , although exports to India , China , and the Middle East have increased . <p> Geopolitical tensions are putting pressure on costs , routes , and market flowsGeopolitical developments add another layer of uncertainty . Conflicts , such as the recent one in the Middle East , often result in higher costs for energy , fertilizers , crop protection products , packaging , and transport . In Peru , prices for imported fertilizers and crop protection products in May 2026 were 18 per cent higher than in February . RaboResearch expects these higher input costs to increase avocado production costs by around 4 per cent year on year , depending on the use of fertilizers , crop protection products , and diesel @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ incurred for transport and packaging materials . The recent conflict in the Middle East has also made the region more difficult to access as an export market . Although the Gulf region ( Bahrain , Kuwait , Oman , Qatar , Saudi Arabia , and the United Arab Emirates ) accounts for less than 1 per cent of global avocado imports , conflicts such as the recent one have disrupted market flows . As a result , Tanzania , for example , has redirected more avocado exports to India , causing supply in that market to increase too quickly and placing downward pressure on prices . <p> ConclusionThe global avocado market continues to expand , including in Europe . However , businesses must prepare for greater uncertainty in volumes , quality , costs , and prices . This requires investment not only in growth , but also in risk management , diversification of supply sources , market planning , promotional activities , and supply chain flexibility . 
@@139060094 <h> Funny <p> The growth of the African leader turns Tangier into a strategic hub for discussing the future of the industry next September <h> The new competitive frontier of the global blueberry market <p> Just two decades ago , Morocco was an emerging player in the blueberry industry . Today it ranks as the world 's fourth-largest exporter , one of the most productive countries per hectare , and a benchmark for genetic innovation , precision agriculture , and logistical efficiency . That rapid transformation is precisely why the upcoming XLIII International Berry Seminar Morocco 2026 , to be held in September in Tangier , will bring together growers , researchers , and companies to examine one of the most dynamic production models in the global market . <p> For years , blueberry growth was associated primarily with countries such as the United States , Chile , and later Peru . The global industry map , however , has continued to shift , and Morocco has firmly established itself as a major new player . <p> Its development has been remarkable . In just over twenty years , the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ world 's fourth-largest exporting power , driven by a combination of foreign investment , genetic innovation , agricultural technology , and a privileged geographic position on Europe 's doorstep . <p> Morocco currently has more than 7,500 hectares planted with blueberries , and official projections point to reaching 10,000 hectares by 2030 , consolidating an expansion that continues to attract international investment . <p> ProductivityThe true measure of competitiveness , however , goes beyond planted area . Morocco 's industry records average yields that compete directly with those achieved by Peru , the world 's leading exporter . <p> In 2024 , exports ranged between 74,000 and 80,000 tonnes , representing year-on-year growth of close to 15% and generating revenues of more than USD 500 million . For 2025 , estimates already projected exports exceeding 82,500 tonnes , with annual volume approaching 95,000 tonnes . Morocco is growing not just in area , but in efficiency . <p> Genetics as a competitive strategyOne of the pillars of the Moroccan model has been the rapid adoption of proprietary genetics . While other countries still maintain significant areas under traditional varieties , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , incorporating breeding programs from the United States , Australia , and Spain as part of a strategy aimed at maximizing productivity , fruit quality , and climate adaptation . <p> This ability to adopt new varieties quickly is one of Morocco 's key competitive advantages over other origins . <p> Logistics as a structural advantageGeographic location is another decisive factor . While South American growers face long sea voyages to reach Europe , Morocco can supply that market within hours or days , cutting logistics costs and improving fruit condition on arrival . <p> Its main commercial window , between January and May , also coincides with a period of lower availability from the Southern Hemisphere , allowing Morocco to capture value before the European season begins . <p> This combination of proximity , quality , and speed explains the strong growth recorded in recent years . <p> A highly technology-driven modelMorocco 's blueberry expansion has also been accompanied by significant technological adoption . Substrate-based hydroponic systems , high planting densities , smart irrigation platforms , and digital monitoring are now standard features of new agricultural projects . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of close to 15.8% in water consumption while maintaining productivity levels . <p> In a country where water availability is one of the main structural challenges , efficient use of the resource has become a central element of the production model . That said , the growth is not without risks . <p> The 2026 season exposed a new vulnerability for the industry . Severe storms with winds exceeding 110 km/h caused damage to macro-tunnels and production infrastructure in February , reducing early estimated volumes by between 15% and 25% . The impact was immediate . May exports fell from 22,257 tonnes in 2025 to just 1,026 tonnes in 2026 , creating a significant supply constraint . <p> As a result , average FOB prices rose from USD 6.86/kg to USD 8.40/kg , reaching highs of USD 10.95/kg during May . <p> The episode made clear that the main risk is no longer water scarcity alone , but also the growing intensity of extreme weather events . <p> A market seeking diversificationAnother strategic challenge is high commercial concentration . Currently , around 84% of Moroccan exports continue to flow to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ dependence on the European market . The industry is therefore actively working to open new destinations . <p> The United States has shown accelerated growth in recent years , while Asia is emerging as the major medium-term target . The future opening of the Chinese market could represent a turning point for the sector 's development . <p> Tangier : a stage for discussing the futureAgainst this backdrop , the upcoming XLIII International Berry Seminar Morocco 2026 , to be held on Wednesday , 16 September at the Royal Tulip City Center Hotel in Tangier , takes on particular significance . <p> More than a technical gathering , the event will provide an opportunity to examine one of the production models that is redefining international blueberry competition . Genetics , productivity , water efficiency , logistics , climate change , and access to new markets will be among the topics shaping the discussion . Understanding what is happening in Morocco today means more than simply learning about a new producing country . It means observing how the global blueberry industry is evolving and identifying the factors that will determine competitiveness @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of Morocco will take place in the northern part of the country , visiting blueberry farms so that growers from different countries can see Morocco 's production techniques and technology firsthand .

@@139094335 <p> The Standard Group Plc is a multi-media organization with investments in media platforms spanning newspaper print operations , television , radio broadcasting , digital and online services . The Standard Group is recognized as a leading multi-media house in Kenya with a key influence in matters of national and international interest . <p> Worldwide , women carry the greatest burden of malnutrition . More than two-thirds of women of childbearing age do n't get enough of at least one micronutrient . <p> These are the vitamins and minerals , such as iron , that the body needs in small amounts to stay healthy . A shortage is often called " hidden hunger " because a woman can eat enough to feel full yet still not have enough essential nutrients . <p> Despite decades of intervention , the prevalence of anaemia has worsened among women , rising from 28% to 31% since 2012 . Anaemia is an iron deficiency condition in which the number of red blood cells or the haemoglobin concentration within them is lower than normal . It mainly affects women and children . <p> The gender gap @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , where 80% of women of reproductive age are affected by micronutrient deficiencies . <p> Why is progress on women 's nutrition stalling ? <p> A multidisciplinary team of experts in global nutrition , public health and equitable food systems , conducted a systematic scoping review of 518 studies across 125 low- and middle-income countries . It identified 143 drivers of what a woman can eat . The result is the first evidence-based framework of women 's food environments , the factors that influence the quality of women 's diets . <p> The central finding of our research was striking : the single most important thing shaping women 's dietary quality is not food availability or household income . It is agency , a woman 's ability to make decisions about food , to control household finances , to manage her own time , and to move freely . <p> Why does a woman in low-resource settings in Africa , Latin America , or the Asia-Pacific not eat enough of certain foods ? Often the answer is that men or older women control what 's spent on food , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Younger women with lower social status face particular barriers : less control over financial resources , less time because of the unpaid work they do , and restricted mobility . <p> Decades of agriculture and nutrition interventions have largely overlooked these realities . The dominant approach has assumed that educating women about healthy eating is enough . Our review revealed that the research has not focused on the greatest need . The numbers bear this out . Across the 518 studies we reviewed , food literacy -- what women know about nutrition -- was the most-studied factor , appearing in 57% of studies . Women 's agency , which our analysis identified as the most important lever , featured in only about a third . <p> Why this matters <p> The implications go well beyond nutrition science . Telling a woman to diversify her diet without addressing the fact that she has limited decision-making or bargaining power is not an intervention . It places the burden of change on the person with the least power to act . <p> This framework also shows that women 's diets are shaped by @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ not personal food preferences or nutrition knowledge alone . Climate change , urbanisation and globalised food systems all affect the ability to eat nutritious foods , and these pressures interact with gender inequalities . When droughts reduce harvests or conflict disrupts markets , women often bear the consequences first . This is because they have less control over household resources and because women tend to put other people 's needs before their own . <p> A household can look food-secure on paper while the woman in it goes without . Our findings reinforce the case for the United Nations ' move to adopt Minimum Dietary Diversity for Women as a new global development indicator as of March 2025 . This indicator is a proxy for whether women are getting the vitamins and minerals they need . <p> Much of what is known about nutrition comes from research conducted in places that are easy to reach : communities near roads or existing health infrastructure . <p> This is called roadside bias , and it is pervasive . Our review laid bare how uneven the evidence base is . Women in remote @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ at risk of poor nutrition , are underrepresented in the evidence base . If programmes are designed around evidence from easy-to-reach populations , they will miss the women who need help most . <p> But the review also found positive patterns . Women with strong social networks , family , friends , and neighbours nearby , often experienced better dietary quality . Those networks helped share the burdens of procuring food , cooking , and caring for children . <p> What should change <p> If agency is the primary lever , then nutrition interventions must be designed around it . <p> Agricultural policies should address barriers to women 's decision-making and bargaining power , and control over income . <p> When designing programmes or making infrastructure decisions , policymakers need to account for women 's time burdens and mobility constraints , including cultural norms around intra-household allocation of food , and that restrict movement and the walkability of roads . <p> Context matters . The relative importance of different barriers varies based on geography , socioeconomic conditions and culture . This means working with communities rather than imposing external solutions @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ requires investment in primary data collection . This is important in partnership with national researchers who bring expertise in language , context and social dynamics . <p> Research in northern Uganda also shows how low-cost tools such as automated mobile phone surveys can reach people where sending a research team is not feasible . <p> As global nutrition funding is reconstructed in the wake of international aid cuts , the decisions made now about what counts as credible evidence will shape what gets built . If the realities that constrain women 's dietary quality are not taken seriously , new interventions will repeat the failures of the old ones . <p> What a woman eats can shape both her health and that of her children for years and generations to come . Improving women 's nutrition was never only a matter of teaching women what to eat . It is also about giving them the power to act on this knowledge . Until programmes take that seriously , progress will keep stalling

The Standard Group is recognized as a leading multi-media house in Kenya with a key influence in matters of national and international interest . <p> As climate change intensifies droughts , floods , displacement and resource competition , Kenya 's Chiefs have emerged as some of the country 's most important local responders -- bridging government and communities , preventing conflict and strengthening resilience where it matters most . <p> While the world debates climate policy in conference halls , Kenya 's Chiefs are already managing its consequences -- one community , one conflict and one crisis at a time . <p> As climate change redraws patterns of migration and resource use , Kenya 's Chiefs are quietly managing the conflicts that emerge long before they become national security crises . <p> Among Kenya 's many public institutions , few are as visible , accessible and consequential as the office of the Chief . Long before policies are implemented or reports @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in the communities they serve . As climate change reshapes livelihoods , migration patterns and local security dynamics , their role has become increasingly important . <p> Climate change is no longer merely an environmental issue . It is a governance challenge , a development challenge and , increasingly , a security challenge . Droughts , floods and environmental degradation are placing unprecedented pressure on communities , particularly in Kenya 's arid and semi-arid lands . Competition over water , pasture and livelihoods is intensifying , while climate-induced mobility is becoming a defining feature of the new reality . <p> Before these pressures become national crises , Chiefs are often the first to respond . <p> In many respects , Chiefs are the closest thing Kenya has to devolved security . Because they are seldom transferred , they accumulate invaluable local institutional memory -- an intimate understanding of community histories , relationships and conflict dynamics that can not be replicated through short-term deployments . Their effectiveness is rooted in a deep understanding of local histories , customs , languages and relationships . They are often the first to identify emerging @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . This combination of local legitimacy , administrative authority and accumulated institutional memory makes them indispensable in managing the growing challenges associated with climate change <p> Chiefs occupy a unique position within Kenya 's governance architecture . As officers of the National Government Administration , they serve as the critical link between national policy and local implementation . They coordinate with County Commissioners , Deputy County Commissioners and Assistant County Commissioners while working closely with county governments , sector ministries , security agencies and development partners . This position enables them to relay local concerns to decision-makers , coordinate responses across institutions and ensure government programmes reach communities effectively . <p> Nowhere is this role more evident than in Kenya 's north-eastern frontier . For years , Chiefs have quietly managed climate-induced mobility and resource pressures across the Kenya -- Somalia border . Even during periods when formal government authority across the border was weak or absent , they maintained channels of communication through clan elders and customary institutions . <p> In many respects , these Chiefs functioned as informal climate diplomats . <p> As recurrent droughts pushed pastoralist communities @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ relied on local knowledge and trusted relationships to manage movement , negotiate access and reduce the risk of conflict . They understood clan networks that transcend national boundaries and worked through respected elders to resolve disputes before they escalated . Long before climate mobility became part of international policy discussions , Chiefs were already managing its consequences on the ground . <p> Their experience offers an important lesson : resilience is not built solely through infrastructure , technology or policy frameworks . It also depends on institutions capable of maintaining social cohesion , coordinating responses and adapting quickly to changing circumstances . <p> Chiefs play a vital role as a one-stop shop for all matters resilience , serving as the primary link between communities , government services , and humanitarian actors . During times of crisis , they are often the first point of contact for citizens , coordinating displacement response , identifying vulnerable households , and facilitating the delivery of humanitarian assistance . Their leadership is equally critical in public health , where they mobilize communities during immunization campaigns and support disease prevention efforts . Chiefs also work closely @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of millions of people who depend on pastoralism and agriculture . By bringing together disaster response , public health , education , livelihood protection , and community mobilization , chiefs provide an integrated , trusted , and accessible platform for building resilience at the local level . <p> They are also increasingly important champions of environmental stewardship . Through tree-growing initiatives , watershed protection efforts and conservation activities , they help communities strengthen resilience while safeguarding the natural resources on which local economies depend . They also work with communities to curb deforestation and unsustainable charcoal burning , recognising that protecting forests is essential for conserving water catchments , restoring ecosystems and reducing climate vulnerability . <p> Technology can connect people , but even the strongest network has limits . Just as mobile technology has transformed disaster preparedness through early warning alerts that enable communities to evacuate before floods , respond to drought conditions and access life-saving information , Chiefs ensure those warnings translate into coordinated local action . Chiefs provide something no mobile signal can replicate : a permanent presence within the community . During droughts , floods , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ remain the trusted point of coordination between citizens , government institutions and service providers . <p> Investing in Chiefs is therefore not merely an administrative matter . It is an investment in climate adaptation , peacebuilding , disaster preparedness and community resilience . <p> More importantly , building the capacity of a Chief is ultimately about building the capacity of a location . Better equipped , better trained and better supported Chiefs strengthen communities ' ability to respond to challenges , seize opportunities and adapt to change . When local capacity grows , national resilience grows with it . <p> As climate change continues to reshape Kenya 's future , the country will need institutions capable of mobilising collective action , coordinating across different levels of government and responding quickly to emerging threats . Few institutions are better positioned to perform that role than the office of the Chief . <p> They may rarely make headlines , but they remain among the country 's most effective public servants : peacebuilders , community mobilisers , informal climate diplomats and first responders to the challenges of a changing climate . <p> They @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ governance . <p> - The writer is a Governance and Communication Expert <p> Support Independent Journalism <h> Stand With Bold Journalism . @@139127681 <h> Diversification pays off as North Rift maize farmers count losses <p> As prolonged dry spells continue to slash maize production across the North Rift , a Uasin Gishu farmer who shifted to high-value , climate-resilient crops is earning nearly Sh1 million annually , offering a practical example of how diversification can cushion farmers against climate change and unstable markets . <p> David Kwambai , 71 , of Kapsoen Village in Moiben Sub-county , abandoned reliance on maize and invested in coffee , avocado and macadamia farming , transforming his four-acre farm into a profitable agribusiness while many maize growers grapple with declining yields . <p> His success comes as agricultural experts and regional leaders urge farmers to reduce dependence on maize by embracing crops that are more resilient to changing weather patterns and command better market prices . <p> Kwambai told the Kenya News Agency ( KNA ) that he began growing coffee in 2015 with about 100 trees before gradually expanding to about 2,000 trees after realizing the crop generated better returns than maize . <p> " I started coffee farming in 2015 with about 100 trees , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Although prices were not very good in the beginning , I realized coffee was still more profitable than maize , " he said . <p> He said his coffee harvest initially averaged between 300 and 400 kilograms but is expected to exceed 1,000 kilograms this season due to improved farm management and favourable prices . <p> Kwambai sold last year 's coffee through a cooperative society at about Sh700 per kilogram , describing cooperative marketing as a key factor in improving farmers ' incomes . <p> " Coffee harvesting takes about two to three months , but with good care , the returns are worth it , " he said , although he identified coffee berry disease as his biggest production challenge . <p> Seeking to spread risk , the farmer later ventured into avocado and macadamia farming after losses in cabbage and passion fruit production caused by disease outbreaks and poor markets . <p> His 500 avocado trees , planted on two acres , produce between 50 and 80 kilograms of fruit per tree , with the produce selling at an average of Sh110 per kilogram . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ demand . We harvest about three times a year , giving us a steady source of income , " he said . <p> Kwambai has also embraced sustainable farming practices by producing compost from fallen avocado leaves , cow dung and urea to improve soil fertility and reduce production costs . <p> However , he cited pests , diseases , cold weather and inadequate irrigation water as the major constraints affecting avocado production . <p> His macadamia enterprise is also showing promising returns . Having planted the crop in 2018 , he harvested about 300 kilograms of nuts last year and sold them at Sh150 per kilogram . <p> " This year I expect even better yields . Macadamia is a long-term investment , but it pays off , " he said . <p> Beyond crop production , Kwambai practices apiculture and livestock farming to diversify his income further . He keeps 10 beehives , six of which are active , producing up to 200 kilograms of honey every three to four months depending on weather conditions . <p> He also rears dairy cows and goats , which provide additional @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the farm . <p> Although he has access to water from a drilled source , Kwambai said he has not fully adopted irrigation , noting that expanding irrigation would significantly improve productivity during prolonged dry spells . <p> He identified marketing as another challenge facing farmers , saying coffee growers benefit from cooperative marketing structures while avocado farmers largely depend on brokers because of inadequate storage and cooling facilities . <p> " We need proper storage and cooling plants to reduce post-harvest losses , especially for avocados , which are highly perishable , " he said , adding that farmers are considering forming cooperatives to strengthen their bargaining power . <p> Kwambai appealed to the county and national governments to subsidize farm inputs and deploy more agricultural extension officers to train farmers on modern production methods , soil testing and climate-smart agriculture . <p> He also encouraged young people to embrace agriculture as a business , saying farming can generate sustainable livelihoods when managed professionally . <p> " Even at my age , I am still actively farming and employing young people . Agriculture has great potential if farmers embrace @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of maize farmers across the region are experiencing reduced yields because of changing weather patterns , highlighting the risks of relying on a single crop and reinforcing the importance of climate-smart agriculture , improved market access and enterprise diversification in building resilient farming systems .

@@139278710 <h> Vihiga : Youth turn waste into economic opportunities <p> Every morning before sunrise , the streets of Vihiga County begin to stir . They are proving that what is thrown away today can become tomorrow 's economic opportunity . <p> Vihiga County is steadily embracing modern waste management practices that aim not only to keep the environment clean but also to create employment , improve public health and contribute to sustainable development . <p> According to Aquila Lwanga , the Vihiga County Environmental Director , in an interview with Kenya News Agency ( KNA ) , effective waste management is no longer optional but an urgent necessity . <p> She explains that the county has experienced increased urbanisation , population growth and commercial activities , all of which have led to a rise in the volume of waste generated daily . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ water sources and degrades the environment . Every resident has a responsibility to ensure waste is handled properly , " she said . <p> Lwanga noted that the county government has strengthened environmental policies and is working closely with stakeholders to improve waste collection , disposal and environmental conservation . <p> She emphasized that public awareness remains one of the most important pillars in achieving cleaner towns and healthier communities . <p> The County Environmental Planning Officer , Philip Angose , during an interview with KNA , who believes planning is critical in ensuring sustainable waste management systems , echoed the sentiments of the environmental director . <p> According to Angose , effective environmental planning begins with understanding where waste is generated , how much is produced and the most efficient methods of collecting , transporting and processing it . <p> " We are integrating waste management into our county development plans because environmental sustainability supports economic development , tourism , agriculture and public health , " he said . <p> Angose said the county also encourages environmental impact assessments for major development projects to ensure they include waste management @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ must embrace sustainability if counties are to cope with increasing waste generation . <p> Beyond policy and planning , practical waste management takes place every day on the ground . <p> The Vihiga County Waste Management Officer , Jonanga Livingston , during an interview with KNA , said he spends much of his time overseeing waste handling activities and educating communities on proper waste disposal practices . <p> He said one of the biggest challenges is waste segregation . <p> " Many households still mix organic waste with plastics , glass and metals . This makes recycling difficult and increases the amount of waste that ends up in disposal sites , " he explained . <p> Jonanga encouraged residents to separate biodegradable waste from recyclable materials before disposal and reminded them that waste management is an individual responsibility and not just for the county alone . <p> He said organic waste can be converted into compost for farming , while plastics , paper and metals can be recycled into new products . <p> According to Jonanga , simple actions at the household level can significantly reduce environmental pollution . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , schools and public places . <p> " When waste is disposed of carelessly , it blocks drainage systems , causes flooding and creates breeding grounds for disease-causing insects and rodents , " he said . <p> As governments and environmental officers continue to improve waste management systems , entrepreneurs are discovering business opportunities within the sector . <p> A circular economy entrepreneur , Justin Kapanga . During an interview with KNA , he said waste is not merely garbage but a valuable resource waiting to be transformed . <p> Kapanga explained that the circular economy encourages the reuse , repair , recycling and remanufacturing of products instead of disposing of them after a single use . <p> " In a circular economy , materials remain in use for as long as possible . We recover value from products instead of throwing them away , " he said . <p> Every day , Collins Idago , a waste collector , moves from one household and business premises to another collecting waste that many people no longer want to see . <p> During an interview with KNA , Idago said his @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in the evening . <p> Despite the importance of his role , Idago said waste collectors face numerous challenges . <p> " Some residents fail to separate waste before collection , while others dispose of sharp objects without proper packaging , exposing collectors to injuries , " he explained . <p> He also pointed to inadequate protective equipment and occasional delays in payment as challenges affecting waste collection services . <p> Nevertheless , Idago said he remains committed to keeping communities clean . <p> He said he believes waste collectors deserve greater recognition because their work directly contributes to public health and environmental conservation . <p> " When waste is collected on time , communities become cleaner and diseases are reduced , " he said . <p> His experience demonstrates that successful waste management depends not only on government policies but also on the dedication of frontline workers . . <p> " Schools , religious institutions and community organisations can also play a significant role in promoting responsible waste management practices , " he stated . <p> Nambisia added that as Vihiga County , the journey toward cleaner towns and healthier communities is already underway . <p> The commitment demonstrated by environmental officers , entrepreneurs , waste collectors and community members offers hope that waste can become part of the solution rather than part of the problem . <p> In Vihiga County , waste is gradually being redefined not as something to throw away , @@139385684 <h> Africa 's green transition must not become a new colonial project <p> Participants at the just concluded Ecofeminism 2026 Convening that took place in Harare , Zimbabwe <p> SHARE <p> Africa stands at the centre of the world 's energy transition . Beneath its soils lie the lithium , cobalt , graphite , manganese , nickel and rare earth minerals that will power electric vehicles , batteries and renewable energy systems across the globe . At the same time , the continent is being celebrated for expanding renewable energy generation and positioning itself as a leader in climate action . <p> Across Africa , communities are increasingly paying the price for a transition that promises to save the planet while sacrificing the very people who have contributed least to the climate crisis . <p> The global shift away from fossil fuels is rapidly becoming another chapter in Africa 's long history of resource extraction -- one where the language has changed from colonialism and development to sustainability and net zero , but the lived experiences of many communities in numerous villages in Africa remain painfully familiar . <p> This @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in Harare , Zimbabwe , where activists , community leaders , researchers and legal experts from across the continent examined what they described as " energy transition extractivism . " <p> In the Kivu region of the Democratic Republic of the Congo and Hanyanya in Bikita in Zimbabwe , communities living near critical mineral deposits continue to face displacement , environmental destruction , labour exploitation and violence as demand for minerals used in batteries and renewable technologies accelerates . Similar concerns are emerging in Mozambique , Uganda , Madagascar and other mineral-rich countries . <p> What is marketed globally as clean energy often begins with dirty extraction . <p> The contradiction extends beyond mining . <p> Kenya is frequently showcased as one of Africa 's renewable energy success stories . More than 90 percent of its electricity comes from renewable sources , making it a model for the continent . But statistics alone tell only part of the story . <p> Behind many geothermal plants , wind farms and other large renewable energy projects are the Masai and Turkana pastoralist and indigenous communities who have lost grazing land , ancestral @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ celebrate megawatts generated , many host communities continue to struggle with expensive electricity , limited access to energy and inadequate compensation . <p> Renewable energy can not simply replace fossil fuels if it reproduces the same patterns of exclusion , land dispossession and inequality . <p> The transition can not be called " just " when those expected to bear its greatest costs have little influence over its design . <p> South Africa 's Just Energy Transition Partnership ( JET-P ) for example illustrates another important lesson . Backed by an $8.5 billion international financing package , the initiative was expected to help the country move away from coal while protecting workers and affected communities . <p> However , it is clear there is an uncomfortable reality : many promises around reskilling , local economic transformation and fair compensation remain unmet because they were political commitments rather than legally enforceable obligations . <p> Communities were left with expectations but few guarantees . <p> This should serve as a warning to governments across Africa negotiating similar climate finance and transition partnerships . <p> Justice can not be built on goodwill alone @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ participation by those whose futures are at stake . <p> Perhaps the most striking conversations at the recent 2026 regional ecofeminist convening in Harare , Zimbabwe centred not on mining or energy projects themselves but on power -- who holds it and who is excluded from it . <p> Communities repeatedly described feeling locked out of decisions affecting their land . Environmental Impact Assessments are often conducted without genuine consultation . Access to critical information is restricted . Contracts are negotiated behind closed doors , excluding communities on whose land are buried these critical minerals . By the time communities become aware of projects , decisions have largely been made . <p> The 2026 regional ecofeminist convening participants called for expanding what has become known as movement lawyering -- an approach where lawyers work alongside communities instead of merely representing them in court . Such lawyers understand that land rights , indigenous identity and environmental justice are not simply legal questions but social , cultural and historical ones . <p> Equally important is investing in community paralegals , strengthening cross-border legal collaboration and recognising indigenous systems of knowledge and justice @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ not be determined solely in boardrooms , ministries or international climate negotiations . <p> It must also be shaped in villages , pastoral lands , mining communities and indigenous territories . <p> Women have a particularly important role to play . <p> Across the continent , women are often the first to experience the impacts of land dispossession , water pollution and environmental degradation . Yet they remain underrepresented in negotiations that determine the future of their communities . <p> If the world genuinely seeks a just transition , it must move beyond measuring success in megawatts installed , electric vehicles produced or tonnes of carbon emissions reduced . <p> Success should also be measured by whether communities remain on their land , whether indigenous peoples retain their rights , whether women participate equally in decision-making , and whether local economies become more resilient rather than more dependent . <p> Africa does not reject the energy transition . <p> It rejects a transition that repeats the mistakes of the past . <p> The continent should not once again become merely a supplier of raw materials while others capture the benefits @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ transition is one that is democratic , community-led , gender-responsive and grounded in human rights . Anything less risks replacing one form of extractivism with another -- this time painted green . <p> The green economy must not become the latest justification for old injustices . Africa deserves an energy future that is not only renewable but also fair . <p> Dr Melania Chiponda is a climate change activist and executive director , Shine Collab , a global feminist network advancing women-led energy access , climate justice , and community power across Africa and the Global South <p> Information You Can Trust : Stay instantly connected with breaking stories and live updates . From politics and technology to entertainment and beyond , we provide real-time coverage you can rely on , making us your dependable source for 24/7 news .
